VI. GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS.
On August 1 an official announcement was mado con-
cerning the new Governmont's bosic economic policies. Those
policios included the following objcctives: improvement
of netionel defonse fccilities through the cutonomous

cnd consumption of important commoditics; thc utilization

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
-34-
ment of self-sufficiency in important daily necessities,
particularly foodstuff:; expansion of heavy industries,
particularly chemical and machine industries; expansion
of communication and transportation facilities and the
establishment of land development plans. These policies
did not represent, on the whole, any significant changes
from previous broad policies announced by the Government
and the general reaction in the press was to emphasize
that the Japanese people desired a program and policies
designed to cope with immediate problems rather than
policies which represented an ideal to be accomplished over
a long period of time.
During the month of August business and industrial
circles in Japan were frankly apprehensive regarding the
futuro course of Government control over their activities
and the possible involvoment with third Powers over the
question of Japanese entrance into French Indo-China. The
share market was generally weak and leading speculative
issues reached their lowost price levels at the end of the
month since the outbreak of the European hostilities.
On August 29 Prime Minister Konoye announced more
rigid price control for the trade in the yon-bloc area, the
oxtonsion of stricter official control over numerous industries
and tho functions of various trade organizations and businoss
concerns. Immediately after this Government announcement,
shares of the Tokyo Stock Exchange, the so-called "barometer"
of the market, dropped from Yon l26 to a record low of Yen
109.

X. MISC ELLANEOUS.
On August 24 the Ambassador was informed by a foreign
bishcp of the Protestant Episcopal Church that an under-
standing had been reached between the gendarmerie and the
senior Japanese Bishop, Naide, under which in order to fore-
stall pressure against the church no foreigners would there-
after be allowed to have charge of a diocese or a church in
Japan and all financial assistence from abroad would be
refused.
This movement agsinst foreign missioncries, it was
believed, resulted fron ar attitude of the Japanese mil-
itery authoritios thst (a) the Christian Church is dissemina-
ting propagandε fevorable to the United States and Great
Britain which dissemination is contrary to netionel policy
and inimical to Japan, (b) finencial support from abroad
is likely to result in the control hy foroign interest of
Japanese

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
-37-
Japanese clergymen receiving such support and (c) all
foreign missionaries a'e potential spies.
This movement began early in August with the arrest
of the leaders of the Salvation Army. It was reported that
the Methodist Church would be next to be "purged" by the
gendarmerie. Leading missionaries were of the opinion that
this movement would mark the end of missionary activities
in Japan.
On August 30 there was reported the meeting of 50
representatives of various Christian organizations in
Japan under the chairmanship of Mr. Abe, President of the
Federation of Christian Churches in Japan. The meeting was
called for the purpose of adopting a common policy for
Christian churchcs in Japan which would be conformeble to
the "new netional structure." It was reported that
efforts would be made to have the various Christian sots
amalgamate into one organization and that all foreign
assistance and influence would be eliminated from Chris-
tian endeavors. The meeting adjourned without achieving by
concrete results but wes scheduled to be reconvened on
September 2 for further discussions of this question as well
as of Christian missionary activities in Mcnchukuo" and
China.

The Honorable

Sir:
With reference to the Department's instruction No. 93
of July 23, 1926, I have the honor to transmit herewith the
Embassy's monthly report on conditions in Japan for the
month of August 1940.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

(c) Arrest of Associated Press Correspondent,
Mr. Relman Morin.*
Mr. Morin, Associated Press correspondent, Tokyo, was
arrested by the Gendarmerie on the afternoon of July 3l and
was released early in the morning of August 1. In discussing
his arrest with members of the Embassy, Mr. Morin said that
the questioning concerned primarily press reports which he

Embassy as being the source of erroneous reports sent to the

alleged disclosure of the British Embassy as the source of
misinformation as evidence that the British Embassy itself
may be the heart of British espionage against Japan". Almost

(e) Reaction in Japan to the American Defense
Program of the Western Hemisphere.*
There was considerable editorial commont following the
conclusion of the joint defense agreoment between the United
States and Canada. The ASAHI of August 20 similar to other
vernacular newspapers stated that the action was a logicel
step and not unexpected but urged that while the agreement
had no direct or immediate relationship with Japan, it was
nevertheless necessery for Japan to watch carefully the
diplomatic activitios of the United States and of Soviet
Russia in the drastic and rapidly changing world conditions.
The HochI asserted that the agreemont affected not only
the Atlantic but also the Pacific, the West Pecific and the
Continents of Austrclia, East Asia and Europe. It was pre-
dicted thet e similer egreement uniting tho American and
Australien Continents might be concluded in the future. The
HOCHI went on to say thet it wcs nov: necessary for the Japa-
nese Government and tho Jepenese people to exert every offort
to acquire and osteblish the "grecter East Asie mutucl prospo-
rity sphere.
On August 22 all vernaculer newspapers commentod on the
report thet Greet Britain would lease naval beses in the

Throughout the month of August there continued to appear
from time to time in the vernacular press articles emphasizing
the seriousness to Japan of continucd development of possiblo
military cooperation between the United States and England.

II. RMLATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES,
(a) Great Britain.
1. Arrest of British subjects n apen aua Japanese
subjects in British Empire.
On August 3 all the vernacular newspapers in Toryo
issued extra editions reporting the arrest of prominent
Japanese in London: This action was considered by the press
to be entirely retaliatory for the arrest of British subjects
in Japan. This original announcement was followed by more
elaborate comment in the press curing the following few days.
The Japanese arrested were said to be prominent businessmen
above any possible suspicion of espionage. Some journals
went so far as to characterize the Biitish acticn as barberous
and illegal. Fear was expressej conccrnins the possibility

(b) The Netherlands East Indies.*
On August 3 it was reported in the press that General
Koiso, former Minister of Overseas Affairs in the first
Konoye Cabinet (1937-1938) had been offered the position
of official delegate from the J apanese Government to the
Netherlands East Indies and that he would be given broad
powers and the highest rank. The purpose of General Koiso's
mission was said to be to establish the closest political
and economic relations between the two areas on the basis
of mutual interest and position within the sphere of greater
East Asia. At the same time certain newspapers reported
that while en route to Tokyo General Koiso had stated, inter
alia, Japan China and Manchukuo" alone were insufficient for
the construction of an economic zone in Greater East Asia;
that it was necessary to include the South Seas area; that
from a moral viewpoint the freeing of the Oriental races was
necessary and was destined or realization; that the Nether-
lands Indies had long been exploited as a colony and had been
subjected to "administrative pressure". General Koiso en-
visaged the possibility of friction with the United States
in the oxecution of this program and said that Japan's re-
solute determination was required.

(f) China.
1. Military Operations in China.
Japanese military operations in China during the month
of August, with the excepticn of aerial cperations, were on
the whole limited to what were cheracterized as "mopping up"
expeditions. More important fighting took place in Central
Hopei and Eastern Shansi in which areas well coordinated and
quite effective raids on railways vere conducted by the Chinese
Eighth Route Army. According to Japanese announcements "mopping
up" operations against those units of the Eighth Route Army
countinued throughout the month of August.
In northeastern Anhwei Province the Japanese Army also
carried out operations against Chinese guerilla forces opera-
ting in that general district.
Naval landing parties during the month of August carried
out a series of minor raids along the South China Coast.
These Japanese marine forces did not attempt to occupy any
territory but merely landed and in raids destroyed some supplies
and then withdrew.
Both thc Japanese Army and Navy air arms continuod their
long: distanco raids on Chungking and other points in Szechuan
Province. In addition to these long-range aerial bombing
oporations, tho Army air forccs bombed the communicetions
lines in Kwangsi Province.

(c) New National Structure.*
On August 28 Prince Konoye issued a statement prior to
the opening of the first session of the Preparatory Com-
mittee on the establishment of the new structure. This
statement in surmary was as follows:
"If Japan is to bring the China Affair to a
successful conclusion and to tako an ective part
in the establishmont of a new world ordoer Japen
must perfect a national defense structure of the
highest degree. The basis of such a structure is
c powerful internal structure. This is indecd a
netionel demand. It is not e domend of a tomporcry
charccter but of a permanent one. Whether or not
Jepan can ostablish such n strong nationcl structure
will decide the very riso or fall of the netiona.
Among the items to be considerod (are) harmonious
cooperation between the High Command and the administra-
tive branch of the Government, the consolidation of
State mechanisms and the heightening of efficiency and
the establishment of a new Parliamentary structure as
an organ for assisting the Throne. The aim of the
new national structure is to unite the total energies
of the state. The organization of the nation must
therefore extend to the economic and cultural spheres.
There must be a solid nation-wide structure in which
all component parts are organized vertically, and
they are also bound together horizontally. A definite
national movement is necessary for the successful
realization of this national structure. Such a
movenent
*3mbasay's tolograms Nos. 759, August 28, 7 p.m.; 760,
August 28, 8 p.m. Despatoh No. 4946, Soptombor 5, 1940.

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
movement should spontaneously spring from the people
themselves. If it is planned or guided by the Govern-
ment, or it is gi:en anedministrative structure, it
may hinder the spontaneous menifestation of the people's
energies. The present circumstances. however, do not
allow us to rely only cn the spontaneous develop-
ment of such a movemont. Mcrcever, agitations from
bolow, are liable to dcgenorete into factionel strifes
and fail to expand into a really national mcvemont. The
Govornment have thus found it nocessary to take posi-
tive steps for fostering and directing this movemont.
This movement is a common undertaking of both the
Government and the people: it is a nation-wide move-
mont to assist the Throne, The new national structure
movement aims at superseding the old party politics
postulated upon liberalism. When such a movement
is led by the Government itself it cannot in any
sense be a party movement. Noither can it be allowed
to tako tho form of a single party system. No matter
what brillient results such a system may have roaped
in other lands, it is not acceptable in Japen bccause
it is contrary to the basic principle of our national
polity of 'One Sovereign over all'. In Japan it
is the privilege of all His Imperial Majesty's
subjects to assist the Throne and that privilege
cannot be monopolized by the power of either a single
individual or a single party. If there should arise
a difference of opinion concerning the assistance
to be offered, the final decision would rest with the
Throne. And once an Imperial decision has been
given, all subjects of the Throne should unite in
obeying His Majesty's word, That is the true essence
of Japanese polity."
Although the sessions of the Preparatory Committee
began there were no significant developments nor was there
any clarification of the "new national structure" during
the month under review.

ment of self-sufficiency in important daily necessities,
particularly foodstuff; expansion of heavy industries,
particularly chemical and machine industries; expansion
of communication and transportation facilities and the
establishment of land development plans. These policies
did not represent, on the whole, any significant changes
from previous broad policies announced by the Government
and the general reaction in the press was to emphasize
that the Japanese people desired a program and policies
designed to cope with immediate problems rather than
policies which represented an ideal to be accomplished over
a long period of time.
During the month of August business and industrial
circles in Japan were frankly apprehensive regarding the
futurc course of Government control over their activities
and the possible involvcment with third Powers over the
question of Japanese entrance into French Indo-China. The
share market was generally weak and leading speculetive
issues reached their lowest price levels at the end of the
month since the outbreak of the European hostilities.
On August 29 Prime Minister Konoye announced more
rigid price control for the trade in the yen-bloc area, the
oxtonsion of stricter official control over numerous industries
and tho functions of various trade organizations and businuss
concerns. Immediately after this Government announcoment,
shares of the Tokyo Stock Exchange, the so-called "barometer"
of the market, dropped from Yon l26 to a record low of Yen
109.

X. MISC ELLANEOUS.
On August 24 the Ambassador was informed by a foreign
bishop of the Protestant Episcopal Church that an under-
standing had been reached between the gendarmerie and the
senior Japanese Bishop, Naide, under which in order to fore-
stall pressure against the church no foreigners would there-
after be allowed to have charge of a diocese or a church in
Jepan and all financial assistence from abroad would be
refused.
This movement against foreign missioneries, it was
believed, resulted fron on attitude of the Japanese mil-
itery authoritics that (a) the Christian Church is dissemina-
ting propagande fevorable to the United States and Great
Britain which dissemination is contrary to nctionel policy
and inimical to Japan, (b) finencial support from abroad
is likely to result in the control hy foroign interest of
Japanese

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
-37-
Japanese clergymen receiving such support and (c) all
foreign missionaries are potential spies.
This movement began early in August with the arrest
of the leaders of the Salvation Army. It was reported that
the Methodist Church would be next to be "purged" by the
gendarmerie. Leading missionaries were of the opinion that
this movement would mark the end of missionary activities
in Jap an.
On August 30 there was reported the meeting of 50
representatives of various Christian organizations in
Japan under the chairmanship of Mr. Abe, President of the
Federation of Christian Churches in Japan. The meeting was
called for the purpose of adopting a common policy for
Christian churchcs in Japan which would be conformable to
the "new national structure." It was reported that
offorts would be made to have the various Christian sots
amalgamate into one organization and that all foreign
assistance and influence would be eliminated from Chris-
ticn endeavors. The meeting adjourned without achieving by
concrete results but wes scheduled to be reconvened on
September 2 for further discussions of this question as well
as of Christian missionary activities in Manchukuo" and
Chine.

restrained in language and tone (page 23).
The average value in September 1940 of

For the first time in almost 15 months

There were beginning to be indications

to establish complete financial independence
and to undertake to Japanize Christianity in
Japan. One step proposed for later considera-
tion was a. merger of Japanese Christian mis-
sions, churches, schools and other religious
organizations (page 36).

(a) Shanghai Defense Sectors.*

Shanghai.

on the contrary considered it a matter of grave importance

very sympathetic and desired to do all in their power they
had come to the conclusion that everything possible had al-
ready been done by the Fore:gn Office to influence the author-
ities at Taiwan and that a further approach by the Ambassador
to the Foreign Minister would be of no avail.* The Minister
for Foreign Affairs, concurrently Minister of Colonies, felt
that he could bring no furtler pressure to bear which would
be effective. On September 1l Matsuo's appeal for retrial
was refused and a threc-year sentence became final. On
Septeriber 21 the Ambassacor, in a conversation with the Minist.
for Foreign Affairs, made the strongest passible ayneal per-
sonally and unofficially, both orally and in a letter to Mr.
Matsuoka, in an effort to have the Minister interest himself
in obtaining a complete pardon for Mr. Matsuo. The Minister
was obviously impressed and replied that he would do his best.

(g) Selection of Ambassador to the United States.*
No progress was made during the month of September in
the selection of a new Ambassador to the United States. Tho
Embassy obtained from time to time reliable rumors of the
position having been offered to various Japanese who had
declined to accept.

The Embassy was informed on September 28 that a senior
member of the Italian Embassy at Tokyo had said in a con-
versation with a thoroughly reliable American citizen that
the word "attacked" as used in Article 3 of the alliance
could be interpreted to include economic sanctions. He weit
on to say that the pact had been intended as a "stop light"
for the United States.
It was obvious that the pact was aimed primarily at the
United States and the advantages to Germany were readily ap-
parent. Japan's gains or advantages were much less patent
but it was believed possible that there were appended to the
pact secret clauses probably applying to one or all of the
following considerations: (a) German and Italian support of
Japan's program vis- à-vis French Indochina and the Netherlands
East Indies; (b) the ability of Germany to induce the coopera-
tion of Soviet Russia in relieving Japan's anxieties in the
north; and (c) possible German intervention, probably with
Russian cooperation, with a view to mediation in the Sino-Japa-
nese conflict. Signing the paot with Germeny and Italy
obviously meant for Japan a momentcus gamble on Germany's
winning the war against Great Britain.
While the vernacular press was unanimous and emphatio
in welooming and approving the conclusion of the tripartite
paot, all editorial comment was reserved and restrained in
language and in tone. The serious consequences which might
follow this step were emphasized with oonsiderable olarity.
The oommon belief and aspiration of Japan, Germany and Italy
to

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
to establish a "new world order" were broadly emphasi zed, A!
newspapers agreed that the Pact involved no change in Japen'
status as a non-belligerent unless and until a third country
attacked one of the signatories. The view that the pact was
not aimed at any one country was unanimously expressed but
at the same time all agreed that the fundamental aims and
polioies of the Japanese Government were thoreby clearly dif-
ferentiatec from those of the American and British Govern-
ments and that this step might lead to eventualities of tha
most serious character.

(a) Canada*
On September 14, as it was entering Tokyo Bay, the
Canadian Facific Steamship TRESS OF ASIA was bombed by a
Japanese naval airplane and several employees were injured.
The Japanese officials were quick to express regret and to
declare that the incident was the result of an unfortunate
accident; that the Canadian Legation would be furnished a
full report; and that tho Japanese Government was prepared
to pay compensation in full for the damage sustained.

(1) China.
(a) Military Operations.*
During the month of September the principal Japanese
military activities in Ncrthorn China centered in Shansi
Province. In August forces from the Chinese Eighth Route
Army had cut several railways in North China, and as a con-
sequence, all through September the Japanese forces devoted
their efforts toward clcaning up th.e areas along the railways.
There were several punitive expeditions convorging on an
area southeast of Taiyuan at Ichengchen. For these activitics
the Japanese employed about three divisions.
In Central China the principal Japanese military ac-
tivity was also reported to be a converging operation against
guerrillas in northeast Anhwei Province, south of Lake Hungtze.
In South China there was reported little or no activity
of importance between the Japanese and Chinose armies. Most
significant development was the movement of the Japanese Army
in Kwangsi whioh from Soptember 22 to the 25th made an at-
tack across the border against Frenoh Indo-Chinese troops
and captured Langson. This activity seemed to mave been
taken

VI.
GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS*.
A continuation was observed of the unfavorable condi-
tions in Japan's general economic situation from last month
such as the further downward trend of the nation's export
trade, further unfavorable effects of the recently introduced
anti-luxury legislation, and a feeling of general uneasiness
over the trend of economic policies of the "new pclitical
structure" as well as the continued uncertainty regarding
possible developments in the international situation. All
0
of these factors contributed to further reduced business ac-
tivity in Japan during September. The stock market in parti-
W
cular reacted to the general unsatisfactory state of affairs
and practically all issues declined markedly during the month.
e
The average value of the leading shares was six percent below
7
the August level and 8.3 percent below the level of the same
C
month in 1939. The Tokyo Stock Exchange estimated that this
slump in stock values was responsible for a declineof over
e
Yen 1,000,000,000 in security values during the month of
September.

Foreign Trade
According to the preliminary figuros of the Finance
Ministry, the total foreign trade of the Japanese Empire
during September was valued at Yen 557,624,000, representing
a deoline of 7.6 peroent in the total foreign trade for the
month of August and 7.3 percent less than the total foreign
trade for September, 1939. Exports were reported to have been
Yen 277,078,000, showing a sharp deoline of 12.7 peroent less
than

VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COM.ERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COLMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
IX. LABOR.
Nothing to report.
X. MISCELLANEOUS.
It was reported on September 3 that a meeting of repro-
sentative Japanese Christian organizations had docided to call
a mass meeting of all Japanese Christians on October 17 to
settle the que stion of the proposed merger of Japanese
Christian missions, churches, schools and other religious
organizations.* At this seme mecting of representatives a
resolution was adopted to the effect that Japanese Christian
organizations should establish complete financial independence.
There were no further concrete developments in this con-
nection during the month of September but considerable appre-
hension was felt by American missionaries in Japan and it
was believod that the Japanese would make a serious effort
to Jipanize Christianity in this country.

(b) Ambassador's interviewswith the Minister and
Vice Minister for Foreicn Affairs.
(1) Ambassador's conversation on October
5 with the Forei gn Minister - Japanese
Government's statement to the United
States regarding the tri-rartite alliance
of Japan, Cermany and Italy.
On October 5 the Minister for Foreign Affairs requested
the Ambassador to call, and in a conversation lasting more
than two hours, Mr. Matsuoka discoursed on a variety of
subjects. At the beginning of the talk he communicated
orally in English and handed to Mr. Grew the Japanese
text of a pronouncement entitled "A Statement to the Unitod
States Concerning the Three Power Alliance".
The statement began with the following paragraph: "The
recent Three Power Alliance is not aimed at any partioular
country

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
country. If Japan, Germany and Italy unite, the probabi-
lity of being attacked by another country is decreased, the
spreading of world disorder may be prevented, and in this
sense the Alliance contributes to world peace. By this
treay Japan has further clarified its intention to estab-
lish a New Order in Greater East Asia including the South
Seas."
The stateiment then repeated the oft-announced thesis
that the New Order means the establishrent of conmon ex-
istence and mutual prosperity of "Greater East Asia"
(formerly East Asia), and declared that the construction of
the New Order would permit Japan "freely to carry on enter-
prise, trade, and emigration in and to eaclh and every land
in Greater East Asia and thereby be enabled to solve its
population problem". This, it was stated, did not, how-
ever, mean that the areas in ouestion "are to be exploited
and oonguered" nor "closed to trade and enterprise of other
countries". Japan's reasonable and peaceful efforts to
solve its population problem, its emigration, trade and
enterprises abroad, the statement asserted, heve been
nullified by various countries of Europe and Americe. In
the Greater East Asia sphere the endeavor is being made to
abolish "such unnatural restrictions on the free activities
of mankind.....through peaoeful means in so far as pos-
sible and with the least possible undesirable ohange in
the statue quo".
With regard to Japan's policy conoerning China, the
statement deolared that, owing to the lack of understanding
on

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.98)
-4
on the part of sone Chinese people and owing to the attitude
taken by England and the United States in not recognizing
"Manchukuo", which cave rise to Chiang Kai-shek's hope of
recovering "Manchukuo", what in fact is a war broke out.
Regardless of this fact and the inevitable effect that war
must have on the rights and interests of third countries,
those powers, the statemont continued, arc not only check-
ing Japan's actions through unwarranted legalistic arguments,
but are oppressing Japan through restrictions on the exporta-
tion of inportant commolities to Japan, and at the same
time are giving positive aid to the Chinese National Govern-
ment. Such actions, it was averred, are di rected at keeping
tho Orient under conditions of disorder as lon: as possible
and of consuminc Japan's national strength. The statement
concluded that "Japan entcred into the defensive alliance
with Germany and Italy for no other purpose t! an to resist
such pressure from the Powers, and there is not the slightest
intention to proceed to attack another country".*
During the further conversation, tihe Foreign Minister
stated that now that the tri-partite alliance was consummated
and off his mind, ho intonded to got to work irmedietely on
the acoumulation of American complaints whioh the Ambassador
had presented to him and to do everything in his power to
olean them up. When the Ambassador was on the point of
departing. Mr. Matsuoka presented an earnest plea that Mr.
Grew

Grew should urge his Government not to impose further

In the course of his talk, Mr. Matsuoka brought up

their interests. Mr. Grew arrested the Minister here in
his talk and stated that he was delighted to hear such
a statement but that in fect, as Mr. Grew had clearly in-
dicated in former alks, Japan had already driven out many
legitimate American interests built up through generations
and was busily engaged in completing the sweep.*

(3) Ambassador's interview on October 9 with
the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs.
On October 9 the Ambassador was urgently called to the
Foreign Office by the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs who
stated that he desi red to see Mr. Grew on behalf of the
Foreign Minister without delay. From Mr. Ohashi's conver-
sation with Mr. Grew it became apparont that the Foreign
Minister was worried (1) by reports that the American
Government had "ordered" the evacuation of American citizens
from the Far East and (2) by reports that the American
Government had decided to declare a total embargo against
Japan. Mr. Grew explai ned with regard to point number one
that no "order" had been issued but that certain advice
had been given as a precautionary measure. He stated with
regard to point number two that he was without information.
Upon Mr. Ohashi referring to tha United States Government's
"embargo" on iron and steel scrap Mr. Grew orally conveyed

(o) Abrogation of treaty for the preservation
of seals in the North Pacific Ocean.
On October 23 the Ambessador was called to the Foreign
Office and handed by the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
an "oral" statement announoing the intention of the Japa-
nese
*Department's telegram No. 383, October 8, 6 p.m.
**Embassy's telegram No. 962, Ootober 9, 7 p.m.

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
nese Government to give notioe to terminate the treaty of
July 7, 19ll, for the preservation of fur seals in the
North Pacific Ocean. The statement deolared that fur seals
in the North Pacifio had numbered some 140,000 at the time
of the conclusion of the convention and had gradually in-
creased until they were now estimated at some 2,200,000;
that both direct and indirect damage inflicted on the
fishing industry by the increase in the fur seals had
become very serious; and that as fishing was one of Japan's
chief industries, the Japanese Government was unable to
allow the convention to continue in force in its present
form. The statement concluded with a declaration of the
intention of the Japanese Government to suggest some prin-
ciples upon which it was prepared to conclude a new agree-
ment. On October 25 the Embassy received a note from the
Foreign Minister giving a formal notice of abrogation of
the treaty and stating at the same time that the Japanese
Government was prepared to conclude a new agreement. In
an addendum to this note certain suggestions were made
for a new agreement regarding the capture of seals, the
distribution of their skins, and a limit of 850,000 seals
in the North Pacific Ooean.*

2. Remarks of the Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs regarding the Tripartite Ailiance
and the attitude of the Japanese Govern-
ment towards the United States.
In spite of the explanations of the Foreign Minister
and the Vice Foreign Minister to the Ambassador concerning
the intent of the Tripartite Alliance with respect to
Japan's attitude towards the United States the Embassy
was apprised of remarks made by the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs to responsible persons in Japan to the
effect that the alliance was aimed directly against the
United States. Mr. Ohashi was guoted as asserting that
the United States ever since the passage of the Exclusion
Clause of the American Immigration Act of 1924 had followed
a policy of hampering Japan's necessary and inevitable
expansion; that Anglo-Saxonism is on the verge of bankruptcy
and will be effectively wiped out in favor of world tota-
litarianism, and that Japan therefore must naturally and
necessarily ally herself with those countries which intend
to establish a new world order.*
3. Press Comment.
The Japanese press, at the beginning of the month, in
commenting upon the Tripartite Alliance dwelt upon its
bearing upon Japan's relations with the United States and
made it known that the alliance had been concluded with
the United States directly in mind to check any American
intervention in Japan's execution of its policies in Greater
East Asia including the South Seas. It was stated that the
United
*Embassy's telegram No. 929, October 2, 7 p.m.

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
United States and Great Britain were the most outstanding
examples of the powers hostile to Japan and aiding the
Chiang Kai-shek regime. The influence of Great Britain,
it was said, in China in particular, and to a lesser
extent in the Far East, was declining and therefore the
United States  had now become the greatest obstacle in the
settlement of the China incident and the fulfillment of
Japan's aims concerning the Far East. It was the United
States now which was following a policy of hampering Japan
and meddling in the Far East. Some speculation was voiced
as to what actions the United States would take in regard
to the conclusion of the alliance. Mention was made that
it might be expected that further embargoes would be placed
against Japan, that the United States would induce the
British Empire to take similar measures, and that the United
States would move to acquire the use of British naval and
air bases in the Pacific. It was felt, however, that it
would be too hasty to suppose that the United States would
go so far as to resort to arms to strengthen its policy of
interference in the Far East. It was stated that if the
United States banned oil shipments to Japan and Great
Britain,prohibited the exportation of important raw mate-
rials, and the two countries made other threatening moves
against Japan, Japan would never acquiesoe but would carry
out its fundamental policies despite any obstacles.*
No mention was made in the Japanese press of the un-
favorable

(b) China - Military Operations.
Immediately follovring the reopening of the Burma Road
bombing operations were reportedly begun against the high-
way by Japanese naval units operating from French Indochina
bases. The suspension bridges across the Mekong River were
claimed to have been destmyed on October 25. Later in
the month the bridge over the Salween River was claimed
to have been damaged or destroyed. It was confidently
asserted that through these operations traffic over the high-
way had been brought to a virtual standstill. It was
asserted that the river gorges were too deep and the current
of the rivers too swift to permit the ferrying of goods
across the rivers. Arperently so confident were the Japa-
nese of having paralyzed traffic over the Burma Road by
the bombing operations that articles in the press published
a special message sent by the Chief of Staff of the Japanese
93.94
Navy to the naval forces concerned with the operations
congratulating them on the success of their aerial raids.
On Ootober 28 it was announced thet it had been deoided
to

-20
to withdraw Japanese forces from Nanning, the town in south-
western Kwangsi Province which hed been held by the Japanese
since its capture on November 24, 1939. It was explained
that this action had been taken in view of the fact that
Nanning has now lost its strategical value inasmuch as
Japanese military forces have obteined superior bases for
operations against southwestern China in French Indochina.
Military operations in general during the month under
review were conducted, as during the previous few months,
on a limited scale. One campaign reportedly was waged in
territory not previously occupied. This operation took
place in a triangle south of Hangchcw, Chekiang Province.
By the end of the month Shaohing and Chuki were claimed to
have been captur ed. Chuki is a village on the Chekiang-
Kiangsi Railway and its reported occupation was evidently
the

during the month; one in Anhwei Province in the Hangchow-
Ningchow sector and the cther in Central Shansi Province.
A number of bombing raids were made on Yunnan Province and
several raids carried out against Chungking.
Little comment appeared in October on Japan's relatins

was aroused as to whether some important new developments

with the Wang Ching-wei regime at Nanking.

Previous to the return of Mr. Kobayashi to Japan the

affected by the Tripartite Alliance. Japan only desires

(e) Russia.
Lieut enant -General Yoshisugu Tatekawa, who was ap-
pointed in September as the new Japanese Ambassador to
the Soviet Union, left Japan en route to Moscow early in
October. The replacement of Ambassador Togo, the former
incumbent at Moscow, by a new ambassador was considered as
presaging the inauguration of a new policy cimed at a readjust-
ment of Japan's relations with Russia. Lieutenant General
Tatekawa stated in a press interview on October 14 given at
Harbin, that since Japan's foreign policy has made a com-
plete change through the conclusion of the Tripartite Al-
liance, negotiations between Japan and the Soviet Unior
will revert to a clean slate.
At the beginning of the nonth and intermittently through-
out October marked attention wes displayed by the Japanese

now in view of, and in line with, the tripartite alliance

this country. It was argued that the conclusion of the

On October 5 the Minister for Foreign Affairs requested
the Ambassador to call, and in a conversation lasting more
than two hours, Mr. Matsuoka discoursed on a variety of
subjects. At the beginning of the talk he communicated
orally in English and handed to Mr. Grew the Japanese
text of a pronouncement entitled "A Statement to the Unitod
States Concerning the Three Power Alliance".
The statement began with the following paragraph: "The
recent Three Power Alliance is not aimed at any partioular
oountry。

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
country. If Japan, Germany and Italy unite, the probabi-
lity of being attacked by another country is decreased. the
spreading of world disorder may be prevented, and in this
sense the Alliance contributes to world peace. By this
treay Japan has further clarified its intention to estab-
lish a New Order in Greater East Asia including the South
Seas."
The stateinent then repeated the oft-announced thesis
that the New Order means the establishrent of comon ex-
istence and mutual prosperity of "Greater East Asia"
(formerly East Asia), and declared that the construction of
the New Order would permit Japan "freely to carry on enter-
prise, trade, and emicration in and to each and every land
in Greater East Asia and thereby be enabled to solve its
population problem". This, it was stated, did not, how-
ever, mean that the arees in question "are to be exploited
and oonguered" nor "closed to trade and entorpri se of other
countries". Japan's reasonable and peaceful efforts to
solve its population problem, its emigration, trade and
enterprises abroad, the statement asserted, have been
nullified by various oountries of Europe and America. In
the Greater East Asia sphere the endeavor is being made to
abolish "such unnatural restrictions on the free activities
of mankind.....through peaoeful means in so far as pos-
sible and with the least possible undesirable ohange in
the status quo".
With regard to Japan's polioy conoerning China. the
statement deolared that, owing to the lack of understanding

Regardless of this fact and thc inevitable effect that war

Remarks of the Vice Minister for Foreign

In spite of the explanations of the Foreign Minister
and the Vice Foreign Minister to the Ambassador concerning
the intent of the Tripartite Alliance with respect to
Japan's attitude towards the United States the Embassy
was apprised of remarks made by the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs to responsible persons in Japan to the
effect that the alliance was aimed directly against the
United States. Mr. Ohashi was quoted as asserting that
the United States ever since the passage of the Exclusion
Clause of the American Immigration Act of 1924 had followed
a policy of hampering Japan's necessary and inevitable
expansion; that Anglo-Saxonism is on the verge of bankruptcy
and will be effectively wiped out in favor of world tota-
litarianism, and that Japan therefore must naturally and
necessarily ally herself with those countries which intend
to establish a new world order.*
3. Press Comment.
The Japanese press, at the beginning of the month, in
commenting upon the Tripartite Alliance dwelt upon its
bearing upon Japan's relations with the United States and
made it known that the alliance had been concluded with
the United States directly in mind to check any American
intervention in Japan's execution of its polioies in Greater
East Asia including the South Seas. It was stated that the
United
*Embassy's telegram No. 929, October 2, 7 p.m.

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
United States and Great Britain were the most outstanding
examples of the powers hostile to Japan and aiding the
Chiang Kai-shek regime. The influence of Great Britain,
it was said, in China in particular, and to a lesser
extent in the Far East, was declining and therefore the
United States : had now become the greatest obstacle in the
settlement of the China incident and the fulfillment of
Japan's aims concerning the Far East. It was the United
States now which was following a policy of hampering Japan
and meddling in the Far East. Some speculation was voiced
as to what actions the United States would take in regard
to the conclusion of the alliance. Mention was made that
it might be expected that further embargoes would be placed
against Japan, that the United States would induce the
British Empire to take similar measures, and that the United
States would move to acquire the use of British naval and
air bases in the Pacific. It was felt, however, that it
would be too hasty to suppose that the United States would
go so far as to resort to arms to strengthen its policy of
interference in the Far East. It was stated that if the
United States banned oil shipments to Japan and Great
Britain prohibited the exportation of important raw mate-
rials and the two countries made other threatening moves
against Japan, Japan would never acquiesoe but would carry
out its fundamental policies despite any obstacles.*
N, mention was made in the Japanese press of the un-

(b) China - Military Operations.
Immediately following the reopening of the Burma Road
bombing operations were reportedly begun against the high-
way by Japanese naval units operating from French Indochina
bases. The suspension bridges across the Mekong River were
claimed to have been destmyed on October 25. Later in
the month the bridge over the Salween River was claimed
to have been damaged or destroyed. It was confidently
asserted that through these operations traffic over the high-
way had been brought to a virtual standstill. It was
asserted that the river gorges were too deep and the ourrent
of the rivers too swift to permit the ferrying of goods
across the rivers. Apparently so oonfident were the Japa-
nese of having paralyzed traffic over the Burma Road by
the bombing operations that articles in the press published
a special message sent by the Chief of Staff of the Japanese
Navy to the naval forces concerned with the operations
congratulating them on the success of their aerial raids.
On Ootober 28 it was announced that it had been deoided
to

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
to withdraw Japanese forces from Nanning, the town in south-
western Kwangsi Province which hed been held by the Japanese
since its capture on November 24, 1939. It was explained
that this action had been taken in view of the fact that
Nanning has now lost its strategical value inasmuch as
Japanese military forces have obtained superior bases for
operations against southwestern China in French Indochina.
Military operations in general during the month under
review were conducted, as during the previous few months,
on a limited scale. One campaign reportedly was waged in
territory not previously occupied. This operation took
place in a triengle south of Hangchew, Chekiang Province.
By the end of the month Shaohing and Chuki were claimed to
have been captured. Chuki is a village on the Chekiang-
Kiangsi Railway and its reported occupation was evidently

coast inland. Two "mcp up" campaigns were also undertaken
during the month; one in Anhwei Province in the Hangchow-
Ningchow sector and the cther in Central Shansi Province.
A number of bombing raids were made on Yunnan Provinee and
several raids carried out against Chungking.
Little comment appeared in October on Japan's relatins
with the occupied territories of China. Some speculation
was aroused as to whether some important new developments
might not shortly occur in this respect when, at the end
of the month. General Nobuyuki Abe returned to Japan follow-

with the Wang Ching-wei regime at Nanking.

(e) Russia.
Lieut enant -General Yoshisugu Tatekawa, who was ap-
pointed in September as the new Japanese Ambassador to
the Soviet Union, left Japan en route to Moscow early in
October. The replacement of Ambassador Togo, the former
incumbent at Moscow, by a new ambassador was considered as
presaging the inauguration of a new policy cimed at a readjust-
ment of Japan's relations with Russia. Lieutenant General
Tatekawa stated in a press interview on October 14 given at
Harbin, that since Japan's foreign policy has made a com-
plete change through the conclusion of the Tripartite Al-
liance, negotiations between Japan and the Soviet Unior
will revert to a clean slate.
At the beginning of the nonth and intermittently through-
out October marked attention wes displayed by the Japanese
press to Japanese-Russian relations. It was declared that
now in view of, and in line with, the tripartite alliance
the position of Russia in the international framework and
its attitude towards Japan had become of utmost concern to
this country. It was argued that the conclusion of the
alliance

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) American Government's suggestion for with-
drawel of Americ ans whoso presence is not
urgently required in the Far East.*
On October 9 the press in Japan prominently featured
the Department's announcement urgently suggesting, in view
of the abnormal si tuation which continues in the Far East,
that American citizens, especiall/ women and children and
men not detained by essential or urgent reasons, should con-
sider the advisebility of returnin. to thc United States.
This notice, cmbraciny the substance of the Department's
basic instruction of Ootober 6 and information contained in
the Departmont's subsequent pertinent cormunications were
brought to the attention of Americans here by publio an-
nouncements end dircct communications from the Consulates
in Japan. It was explained to inquirers that the notice

(b) Ambassador's interviewswith the Minister and

(1) Ambassador's conversation on October
5 with the Forei gn Minister - Japanese
Government's statement to the United
States regarding the tri-rartite alliance
of Japan, Cermany and Italy.
On Ootober 5 the Minister for Foreign Affairs requested
the Ambassador to call, and in a conversation lasting more
than two hours, Mr. Matsuoka discoursed on a variety of
subjects. At the beginning of the talk he communicated
orally in English and handed to Mr. Grew the Japanese
text of a pronouncement entitled "A Statement to the Unitod
States Concerning the Three Power Alliance".
The statement began with the following paragraph: "The
reoent Three Power Alliance is not aimed at any partioular
oountry

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
country. If Japan, Germany and Italy unite, the probabi-
lity of being attacked by another country is decreased, the
spreading of world disorder may be prevented, and in this
sense the Alliance contributes to world peace. By this
treay Japan has further clarified its intention to estab-
lish a New Order in Greater East Asia including the South
Seas."
The statement then repeated the oft-announced thesis
that the New Order means the establishrent of common ex-
istence and mutual prosperity of "Greater East Asia"
(formerly East Asia), and declared that the construction of
the New Order would permit Japan "freely to carry on enter-
prise, trade, and emigration in and to each and every land
in Greater East Asia and thereby be enabled to solve its
population problem". This, it w s stated, did not, how-
ever, mean that the areas in question "are to be exploited
and conquered" nor "closed to trade and enterpri se of other
countries". Japan's reasonable and peaceful efforts to
solve its population problem, its emigration, trade and
enterprises abroad, the statement asserted, have been
nullified by various countries of Europe and America. In
the Greater East Asia sphere the endeavor is being made to
abolish "suoh unnatural restrictions on the free activities
of mankind.....through peaoeful means in so far as pos-
sible and with the least possible undesirable ohange in
the status quo".
With regard to Japan's policy concerning China. the
statement deolared that, owing to the lack of understanding
on

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.98)
-4
on the part of some Chinese people and owing to the attitude
taken by England and the United States in not recognizing
"Manohukuo", which cave rise to Chiang Kai-shek's hope of
recovering "Manchukuo", what in fact is a war broke out.
Regardless of this fact and the inevitable effect that war
must have on the rights and interests of third countries,
those powers, the statemont continued, aro not only check-
ing Japan's actions throuh unwarranted legalistic arguments,
but are oppressing Japan through restrictions on the exporta-
tion of inportent commodities to Japan, and at the same
time are giving positive aid to the Chinese National Govern-
ment. Such actions, it was averred, are directed at keeping
tho Orient under conditions of disorder as long as possible
and of consuming Japan's national strength. The statement
concluded that "Japan entured into the defensive alliance
with Germany and Italy for no other purpose than to resist
such pressure from the Povors, and there is not the slightest
intention to proceed to attack another country".*
During the further conversation, the Foreign Minister

olean them up. When the Ambassador was on the point of

Government was prepared to conclude a new agreement. In
an addendum to this note certain suggesti ons were made
for a new agreement regarding the capture of seals, the
distribution of their skins, and a limit of 850,000 seals

2. Remarks of the Vice Minister for Foreign

In spite of the explanations of the Foreign Minister
and the Vice Foreign Minister to the Ambassador concerning
the intent of the Tripartite Alliance with respect to
Japan's attitude towards the United States the Embassy
was apprised of remarks made by the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs to responsible persons in Japan to the
effect that the alliance was aimed directly against the
United States. Mr. Ohashi was guoted as asserting that
the United States ever since the passage of the Exclusion
Clause of the American Immigration Act of 1924 had followed
a policy of hampering Japan's necessary and inevitable
expansion; that Anglo-Saxonism is on the verge of bankruptcy
and will be effectively wiped out in favor of world tota-
litarianism, and that Japan therefore must naturally and
necessarily ally herself with those countries which intend
to establish a new world order.*
3. Press Comment.
The Japanese press, at the beginning of the month, in
commenting upon the Tripartite Alliance dwelt upon its
bearing upon Japan's relations with the United States and
made it known that the alliance had been concluded with
the United States directly in mind to check any American
intervention in Japan's execution of its policies in Greater
East Asia including the South Seas. It was stated that the
United
*Embassy's telegram No. 929, October 2, 7 p.m.

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
United States and Great Britain were the most outstanding
examples of the powers hostile to Japan and aiding the
Chiang Kai-shek regime. The influence of Great Britain,
it was said, in China in particular, and to a lesser
extent in the Far East, was declining and therefore the
United States : had now become the greatest obstacle in the
settlement of the China incident and the fulfillment of
Japan's aims concerning the Far East. It was the United
States now which was following a policy of hampering Japan
and meddling in the Far East. Some speculation was voiced
es to what actions the United States would take in regard
to the conclusion of the alliance. Mention was made that
it might be expected that further enbargoes would be placed
against Japan, that the United States would induce the
British Empire to take similar ieasures, and that the United
States would move to acquire the use of British naval and
air bases in the Pacific. It was felt, however, that it
would be too hasty to suppose that the United States would
go so far as to resort to arms to strengthen its policy of
interference in the Far East. It was stated that if the
United States banned oil shipments to Japan and Great
Britain prohibited the exportation of important raw mate-
rials and the two countries made other threatening moves
against Japan, Japan would never acquiesoe but would carry
out its fundamental policies despite any obstacles.*

(b) China - Military Operations.
Immediately following the reopening of the Burma Road
bombing operations were reportedly begun against the high-
way by Japanese naval units operating from French Indochina
bases. The suspension bridges across the Mekong River were
claimed to have been destoyed on October 25. Later in
the month the bridge over the Salween River was claimed
to have been damaged or destroyed. It was confidently
asserted that through these operations traffic over the high-
way had been brought to a virtual standstill. It was
asserted that the river gorges were too deep and the current
of the rivers too swift to permit the ferrying of goods
across the rivers. Apparently so oonfident were the Japa-
nese of having paralyzed traffic over the Burma Road by
the bombing operations that articles in the press published
a special message sent by the Chief of Staff of the Japanese
Navy to the naval forces concerned with the operations
congratulating them on the success of their aerial raids.
On Ootober 28 it was announoed that it had been deoided
to

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
-20
to withdraw Japan ese forces from Nanning, the town in south-
western Kwangsi Province which hed been held by the Japanese
since its capture on November 24, 1939. It was explained
that this action had been taken in view of the fact that
Nanning has now lost its strategical value inasmuch as
Japanese military forces have obtained superior bases for
operations against southwestern China in French Indochina.
Military operations in general during the month under
review were conducted, as during the previous few months,
on a limited scale. One campaign reportedly was waged in
territory not previously occupied. This operation took
place in a triangle south of Hangchcw, Chekiang Province.
By the end of the month Shaohing and Chuki were claimed to
have been captured. Chuki is a village on the Chekiang-
Kiangsi Railway and its reported occupation was evidently

(a) Netherlands East Indies.
It will be recalled that at the beginning of September*
a Japanese economic mission headed by the Minister of Commerce
and Industry, Mr. Ichizo Kobayashi, was despatched to
Batavia to negotiate a comprehensive trade agreement with
the Netherlands East Indies. A number of meetings were
held during the first part of the month.
It was then an-
nounced that on October 22 Mr. Kobayashi would return to
Japan temporarily, in order that he might attend the cere-
monies celebrating the 26ooth anniversary of the founding
of the Japanese Empire and in order to report the progress
of the negotiations and to consult with the Japanese Govern-
ment. In a statement issued by Mr. Kobayashi on his depart-
ure from Batavia he declared that the negotiations were
making good progress and were expected to enter the second
stege in the near future; that he was fully convinced
of eventual success; and that a most friendly atmosphere
had surrounded the discussions. In a statement made by
the spokesman of the Foreign Office on October 23, Mr.
Suma denied that an agreement regarding petroleum had been
reached by Japan with the Netherlands East Indies authori-
ties and added that the Japane se delegation was still
negotiating on various matters incl uding petroleum and
that the discussions were proceeding smoothly. The
denial of Mr. Suma with regard to the conolusion of an
agreement

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)

agreement regarding the supply of petroleum to Japan from
the Netherlands East Indies was made in reply to inquiries
as to the veracity of rumors to the effect that an arrange-
ment had been reached whereby Japan would obtan approximately
40 percent of its imports of petroleum products from the
Netherlands East Indies.*
Previous to the return of Mr. Kobayashi to Japan the
Japanese press published a report under a Batavia date-
line of a "joint communiqué" issued on October 18 by the
Japanese and Netherland East Indies delegations. In this
communiqué the Japanese delegates made a formal statement
declaring that "regardless of the existence of the Tripartite
Alliance Japan earnestly desires to maintain and promote
friendly relations with the Netherlands East Indies and
is confident that those relations are not in the least
affected by the Tripartite Alliance. Japan only desires
to maintain her relationship of mutual existence and pros-
perity with neighboring countries".**
Resumés of the proceedings of the meetings confidential-
ly furnished to the Embassy set forth the following ma jor
points in the negotiations during the month: The Japanese
delegation presented proposals specifying the desired
quantities of certain products which Japan wished to obtain
from the Netherlands East Indies, including petroleum, tin,
rubber, bauxite, castor seeds and cinchona bark. The Japa-
nese delegation requested a minimum supply of 3,750,000 tons

petroleum from abroad. Apparently no definite agreements
had been reached wi th regard to the other commodities which
Japan desired to obtain from the Netherlands East Indies.*

(e) Russia.
Lieut enant -General Yoshisugu Tatekawa, who was ap-
pointed in September as the new Japanese Ambassador to
the Soviet Union, left Japan en route to Moscow early in
October. The replacement of Ambassador Togo, the former
incumbent at Moscow, by a new ambassador was considered as
presaging the inauguration of a new policy cimed at a readjust-
ment of Japan's relations with Russia. Lieutenant General
Tatekawa stated in a press interview on October 14 given at
Harbin, that since Japan's foreign policy has made a com-
plete change through the conclusion of the Tripartite Al-
liance, negotiations between Japan and the Soviet Unior
will revert to a clean slate.
At the beginning of the nonth and intermittently through-
out October marked attention wes displayed by the Japanese
press to Japanese-Russian relations. It was declared that
now in view of, and in line with, the tripartite alliance
the position of Russia in the international framework and
its attitude towards Japan had become of utmost concern to
this country. It was argued that the conclusion of the
alliance

alliance gave the United States and Great Britain cause to
pause and reflect cn the world political situation. Russia
on the other hand was to be considered by the alliance

with the other two signatories of the alliance, should ad-
vance towards the attainment of Soviet understanding and
cooperationwith the alliance. To do so it was proper for

In the foregoing described press discussions mention

and to induce Russia to enter their camp. The need to
combat such an eventuality was, therefore,an additional
reason for the successful and urgent conclusion of a

Tokyo's despatoh 5321, January 23, 1941,
ubject Roport of Conditions in Japan During
the Month of December 1940".
In addition to items reported elsewhere,
the despatch states that:
Prominence was given in the vernacular
press to the announcement of the pronosed
establishment of a Central Bank of the Hew
National Government at Nanking.
The German colony in Japan continues to
increase, and e0 feature end "cultursl" films
will shortly be imported from Germany.
Wide powers over industry are to be iven
to the Imperial Rule Assistance Association
but modifications in the program may result
from opposition by business interests.
General economic end financial conditions:
The despatch reports thet:
National bonds totallin: Y1,300 million
were offered durinr December but thet only
30 percent were absorbed. Bonde floated during
1940

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
40 were ¥6,684 m1ll1on and in 1939 Y5,298
111on.
Note 1ssue on December 31, 1940 was almost
6,000 million and was 1,000 mill1on above the
figure for last year.
New regulations controlling Japanese ex-
port trade were promulgated.
Intensified steel activity to offset
American embargoes is noted.
Industrial expansion witnessed for the
past three years has come to a helt and output
in both industry and agriculture in 1940 was
below 1939.
The price level showed a downward trend
after September 1940.
Although the yen value of Japanese foreign
trade during 1940 represented an increase in
both exports (1 percent) and imports (19 per-
cent) over l939, it is estimated that the actual
value of both exports and imports was lower in
1940 than in 1939 due to the lowered value of
the yen and higher prices.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Speech of the Foreign Minister, Mr. Matsuoka
before the America-Japan Scciety: on Decenber
19,1940.
On December 19, the America-Javan Society held a luncheon
in honor of Admiral Kichisaburo Nomuru, the newly anpointed
Japanese Ambassador to the United Sttes and  Mr. Horinouchi,
retiring Ambassador, and Mrs. Eorinouchi. The function, at
which Mr. Grew presided, was attended by a number of important
Japenese personages, and was perhaps the largest gathering of
Japanese and American members ever to assemble at a meeting of

II. RELATIONS WITH OTIER COUNTRTES.
(a) Great Britain.
(1) German raiders in the Pacific Ocean.
On Decomber 5, the Norwegian ship "Ole Jacob" whish had
been captured on her way from Singopore to Colombo by a German
raider arrived at Kobe under a Germen prize crew. Ther: its
Norwegien crew and tho crew of another Norwegian ship that hcd
boen captured and sunk in the Indian Occan, were transferred
aboard the German merchnt liner "Scharnhorst" end eventually
freed. The "Ole Jacob" romaincd in port 36 hours and departed.
The arrival of this captured ship in Japan and tho move-
ments of a number of other Gerirn ships in Japanose waters gove
rise to suspicion that arrangoments wore being made either to
fit out a German raider or that Japanese ports werc being used
as beses from which to supply and provision Germon raidcrs in
the Pacific Ocoan.
Tho British Embassy made sevoral approaches to the Japanese
Foroign Office in regard to this matter. In roply to the British
Ambassador's inquirios the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
stated that the "Ole Jacob" had cntercd Kobe for supplies and
thnt upon investigation no ovidence had boen f ound that she had
been used as a rcidor or a supply ship to a reider. The British
Ambassador mde full reservations under international law as to
the use of ports of noutral countries by vessols of belligerent
countrios engaged in military activitios. He also pointed out
practioal

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
practical means by which Japan might prevent such activities
as might result in an impairment of continued Allied shipping
to and from Japan and might possibly force Great Britain to
take precautionary measures with respect to Japanese vessels
in the Pacific Ocean, particularly in view of the fact that
German raiders had been believed to be using as disguises the
names and markings of Japanese ships. The Ambassador was in-
formed that the Japanese Government had no intention to permit
raiders to operate in Japanese waters or to be supplied from
Japanese territory.*
(2) British loan to the Chinese National
Government.
It will be recalled that the British Government in the
early part of December announced that a British loan of
fl0,000,000 was being made to the Chinese National Government.
On December 14, the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs brought
up the question of this loan during a conversation with the
British Ambassador. Mr. Ohashi strongly denounced the measire,
characterizing it as a further indication of the joint efforts
on the part of Great Britain and the United States to weaken
Japan, and he asserted that the declared intentions of the
British and American Governments initiating these actions in
the interests of China were mcre pretext and camouflage for the
implacable hostility of the United States and Great Britain
toward Jepan.**

Amongst these were the action of Japan in allying itself with

only be achieved at the expense of British and other interests
over a wide area; the biased presentations of European war news
in the Japanese press; the wholesale arrest of British subjects
last summer; and the increase of Japanese efforts to provide
Germany with materials vital to the prosccution of the war. In
reply to the Ambassador's observations the Foreign Minister
repeatedly stated that Japan, as the ally of Germany and Italy,
was now bound to give some assistance to their cause and that
he feared that some of the actions which Japan would have to
take might appear to exceed neutral obligations as defined under
international law. Japan, he said, had no intention, however,
of becoming involved in the war and he hoped that such a possi-
bility between the two countries could be avoided. The Minister
then repeated remarks that he had made to Sir Robert Craigie
on November 9, 1940,* to the effect that the Tripartite Alliance
had been entered into for the purpose of deterring the United
States from entering the war or from taking some serious provoca-
tive step such as a visit of a powerful American squadron to
Singapore. The British Ambassador stated that he hoped that
the contemplated Japanese assistance to the Axis referred to by
the Foreign Minister might not be of such character as to be
incompatible with the normal obligations of a neutral under
international law and that the relations between the two countris
might be kept on a peaceful and rational basis.**

(b) Reform of Civil Service System.
On December 3l, the Privy Council finally gave its ap-

Service system, and an ordinance enbodying the plan was sub-
mitted to the Emperor. For a number of years the Privy
Council had consistently declined to assent to the demands
mede by previous governmonts for the revocation of the lew
guaranteeing the status of government officials. Under the
new ordinance th: Gov :rnment would be able to remove any
official from Government office, but it was understood that
the Privy Council had Eiven its consent to the change only
upon the assurance of the Cabinet that the Government would
refrain from promiscuously making removals or appointments.

GOVERIILENT FINANCE.
National bond issues in the amount of Yen 1,300,000,000
were offered durinf the month under review. The absorption
of the bonds was reported to have been exceptionally low, a-
mounting to less than 30 per cent. This noor rate may in
part be attributed to the very large amount of bonds issued,
and also in part explained by the fact that money was in
strong demand by private business in connection with the year-
end activity. Totel Government bonds floated during the year

VI. GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC COINDITIONS*.
There was a decided increase in business and commercial
activity during December. The increase, however, was believed
to be almost entirely seasonal in view of the distribution of
year-end bonuses, year-end financial settlements, and heavy
retail sales in connection with the New Year's holidayi. A
TTT
sharp drop in the stock market occurred in the beginning of
ASURY
the month and prices of all securities were depressed through-
out the period. The slump was attributed to the realizaticn
that the optimism which had prevailed during November on the
2
grounds that the Government would introduce measures to alle-
Co
viate the tight money market and to moderate economic poli-
cics was unwarranted. The money market firmed appreciably
towards the end of tho month experiencing the first rise in over-
W
night call-rates in five months - from 2.56% to 2.74%. Due to the
disbursement of huge Government funds and the accommodations
T
extended by the Bank of Japan the yoar-end settlements were
setisfactorily effected.

General Economic Situation for the Year 1940.
Although the yeer 1940, as far as information and figures
available would show, represented a period of high industrial,
cormercial and agricultural activity, it witnessed a halt to the
trend of inoreased and expanding production of the previous
tnree years and a decline. in prosperity, The high level of
business

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.98)
-37-
business activity apparent last year which continued into js≤C.
perhaps till the middle of the year, in any event gave way
during the last half of the year when definite signs of wea-
kening and contiaoon arpeared
Both industrial and agrlcul-
tural output were pel ow that for the previs ar.
OMME
The volume of industrial production was estimated to have
declined by something in the neighborhood of 5 per cert although
its value in fact showed an increase as a result of the nigh-
RC
er price level during the year. The causes for this decrease
were principally attributed to marked shortages of raw mate-
7
rials for both heavy and light industries, the shortag: of
electric power during the early part and the closing months of
the year, and, with respect to light industries particularly,
the shrinkage of foreign markets for the manufactures. The
continued expansion of thc country's industrial production
capacity was, however, generally maintained, especially in the.
iron and steel, non-ferrous metal, machine tool and chemical
industries, but the rate of increase was considered to have
slowed dowm from that of the previous year.
The Government's statistics for the rice crop of Japan
Proper for the year was 60,874,000 koku (the koku is approxi-
AGRICULTURE-
mately 5 bushels) represonting a 1l.7 per cent decrease as
compared to the crop of last year and a 6.6 per cent decrease
compared with the average during the last five years. The
barley crop also decreascd but the wheat crop was much reater
than that for thc previous year and was the largest ever harvest-
ed. The l940 coccon output was offic ially reported to have
amounted to 87,540.000 kwan (one kwan oqualing 8.3 pounds) which
although
n above the average for the past five years represented
a decline of 3.6 per cent from that of 1939. The value of this
orop, according to the Government figures, was about the same as
that of 1939, despite the drop in raw silk prices from the poak
of

during the latter part of 1939, was checked in 1940 and showed

On December 19, the America-Janen Society held a luncheon
in honor of Admiral Kichisaburo Nomura, the newly appointed
Japanese Ambassador to the United Statcs and f Mr. Horinouchi,
retiring Ambassador, and Mrs. Lorinouchi. The function, at
which Mr. Grew presided, was attended by a number of iaportant
Japanese personages, and was perhaps the largest gathering of
Japanese and American members ever to assemble at a meeting of
the Scciety.
Several of the hirhest Jepanese navel crficers
wort pregent.
The Minister Por Foreim Aafairs, Mr. Yosuke
Matseora. dolivered a speech  vhe unehecn. on Anerican-
Japanese aftail: in w ich be propounded the position of Japan
vis-à vis the United Stutes ader the present axioms of Jananese
foreign plicy
In summary tiho sai tent poiuts ef the speech were as follows:
In beginning the stbetantive part of the address the Foreign
Minister etated thet is must to odmittel that the relations
botween Japan and the United States were severely strained.
The fundamental cause for this, he declared, was "American
misapprehension of Juran's aims and acpirations". He asserted
that sontrers, hovever, to irpressions current in America and
elsewhore Jupan vas engagel not in in imperialistic war of
greed and sgsreasien i. China bu's in a moral crusade, endeavor-
ing to initiate an era f sndu ring peace in Greater East Asia.
The basic principles of Japan stood unalterably for peace and
order. Japan "shut the door nuwhere and to none", and welcomed
any nation that desired to take a hand in the task at hand,
but

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
but upon the understand ing that under the "New Order" there
should be "no conquest, no oppression, no exploitation" by
those nations as well as on the part of Japan itself.
The Minister next spoke of Japan's ains to create
regional unity. He stated that the world was "too wide poli-
tically and too narrow economically"; that economic activity
should be world-wide and without limitation but that political
efforts should be restricted to only those spheres in which
a nation is vitally interested; and that if regional peace
were effectively secured tre world, as a whole, would be able
to enjoy universel peace. He pointed to the recent Havana
Conference as a case in point.
Then taking up the question of the Tripartite Alliance he
asserted that Japan herbored no hostile intention thereunder
toward America. "Jupan", he said, "only desires to be left
elone so that it may carry on its constructive work unhindered
and on the othor hand to see the trouble in Chinc and the war
in Europe brought speedily to an end without adding more parti-
cipants, particularly such a powerful one as America." He
categorically stated that Japan would remain "loyal to her Allies
and that Jepan's foreign policy "will revolve, in the future,
around the Three Power Pact as its pivot."
Referring to the "China problom", he termod the fate of
China as "largely a question of sentiment to thc Americans" but
an issuo vital to the oxistence of Japen. He said that Japan
was not without hope as to an early restoration of peace in
China" which would automatically liquidate tro tension betweon
Japan and America. He said that the new Chinese Central Govern-
ment representod a "harbinger of botter times" and he callod
upon

upon the United States to understand the broad aspects of the

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
(1) German raiders in the Pacific Ocean.
On Decomber 5, the Norwegian ship "Ole Jacob" which had
been captured on her way from Singopore to Colombo by a Cerman
raider arrived at Kobe under a Gormen prize crew. Therc its
Norwogian crew and tho crew of another Norwegian ship that had
been captured and sunk in the Indian Occan, were trancferred
aboard the German merchnt liner "Scharnhorst" end eventually
freed. The "Ole Jacob" ronaincd in port 36 hours and departed.
The arrival of this captured ship in Japan and tho move-
monts of a number of other Germrn ships in Japanose waters gove
rise to suspicion that arrangoments were being made cither to
fit out a German raider or that Japaneso ports werc boing used
as beses from which to supply and provision German raidcrs in
the Pacific Ocoan.
Tho British Embassy made sevoral approaches to the Japanese
Foroign Offico in regerd to this matter. In roply to the British
Ambassador's inquirios the Vico Minister for Foreign Affairs
stated that the "Ole Jacob" had cntercd Kobe for supplies and
thnt upon invastigation no ovidence had boen f ound that she had
been usad as a rcidor or a supply ship to a reider. The British
Ambassador mde full reservations under international law as to
the use of ports of noutral countries by vessols of belligerent
countrios ongaged in military activitios. He also pointed out
praotical

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
practical means by which Japan might prevent such activities
as might result in an impairment of continued Allied shipping
to and from Japan and might possibly force Great Britain to
take precautionary measures with respect to Japanese vessels
in the Pacific Ocean, particularly in view of the fact that
German raiders had been believed to be using as disguises the
names and markings of Japanese ships. The Ambassador was in-
formed that the Japanese Government had no intention to permit
raiders to operate in Japanese waters or to be supplied from
Japanese territory.*
(2) British loan to the Chinese National
Government.
It will be recalled that the British Government in the
early part of December announced that a British loan of
£l0,000,O00 was being made to the Chinese National Government.
On December 14, the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs brought
up the question of this loan during a conversation with the
British Ambassador. Mr. Ohashi strongly denounced the measure,
characterizing it as a further indication of the joint efforts
on the part of Great Britain and the United States to weeken
Japan, and he asserted that the declared intentions of the
British and American Governments initiating these actions in
the interests of China were mere pretext and camouflage for the
implacable hostility of the United States and Great Britain
toward Jepan.**
(3) General Relations.
During the month under review the British Ambassador held
several conversations with the Foreign Minister in which he
took up the general question of Anglo-Japanese relations. He
noted
the unfavorable actions and attitude of Japan towards
Great Britain, its nationals and their interests which had led
to
mbassy's telegram No. 1301, Dec. 9, 10 a.m.; 1312, Dec. 10, 8
pem.; 1s17, Dec. 1l, 7 p.m. Despatoh from American Consul, Kobe.
