to the impairment of the relations between the two countries.
Amongst these were the action of Japan in allying itself with
Britain's enemies; Japan's program of expension which could
only be achieved at the expense of British and other interests
over a wide area; the biased presentations of European war news
in the Japanese press; the wholesale arrest of British subjects
last summer; and the increase of Japanese efforts to provide
Germany with materials vital to the prosecution of the war. In
reply to the Ambassador's observations the Foreign Minister
repeatedly stated that Japan, as the ally of Germany and Italy,
was now bound to give some assistance to their cause and that
he feared that some of the actions which Japan would have to
take might appear to exceed neutral obligations as defined under
international law. Japan, he said, had no intention, however,
of becoming involved in the war and he hoped that such a possi-
bility between the two countries could be avoided. The Minister
then repeated remarks that he had made to Sir Robert Craigie
on November 9, 1940,* to the effect that the Tripartite Alliance
had been entered into for the purpose of deterring the United
States from entering the war or from taking some serious provoca-
tive
e step such as a visit of a powerful American souadron to
Singapore. The British Ambassador stated that he hoped that
the contemplated Japanese assistance to the Axis referred to by
the Foreign Minister might not be of such charactor as to be
incompatible with the normal obligations of a neutral under
international law and that the relations between the two countries
might be kept on a peaceful and rational basis.**

Editorial comment in Japan regarding the conclusion on
November 30 of the treaty between Japan and the "New Crinese
National Government" at Nanking was headed by a long treatise
on the subject issued by the Domei News Agency.
It deseribed
the treaty as laying the foundation for the "Mutual Prosperity
Sphere in Greater East Asia". It then devoted its main atten-
tion to what it stated was the "international significance that
was to be attached to the event". It declared in this regard
that an end had been put to that phase of Chinese history
during whioh China had been denendent upon Great Britain and
the United States and that the shackles which those two countries
had imposed on China since the Opium liars a hundred years ago
were at last broken. Henceforth, China, it held, would asso-
ciate itself with the signatories of the Tripartite Alliance
and could count upon the material and other support of the
Axis Powers. The conclusion of the treaty further sicnified
more than anything else, it stated, the joint co-operation of
China and Japan against the nations that tried to stand in the
way of the construction of the "New Order in East Asia" and
also joint endeavors for an early materialization of an "East
Asia Co-prosperity Sphere" as a preliminary step towards the
establishment of a new world order.
The vernacular press accorded an enthusiastic welcome to
the treaty. Typical of editorial comments were such statements
that. "Sino-Japanese bonds had been tied into an inseparable
knot" and that a medium had been found whereby the disposal of
the China Incident would certainly bo accelerated. Much was
also made in the comments of the fact thet Japan "did not desire
to tako an inch of foreign territory nor to demand a single
oent in payment for tho last three and one half years of
hostilitios" as demonstrating the just and equitable attitude
of Japan towards China. Togother with these comments the

(2) Military operations.*

Lin Hsien and Hing Hsien. In the Nanchang area the Japanese
foroes made a small scale offensive resulting in the capture

(o) Thailand.
On December 28 the treaty of amity between Japan and Thai-
land, signed on June 12, 1940, was promulgated and published in
the

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)
-26-
the Japenese Official Gazette. The treaty provided (1) for the
mutual respect of the territorial integrity of cach country and
the reaffirmation of peace and friendship existing between the
countries; (2) for the exchange of information cnd for con-
sultation on any question of common interest that might arise;
and (3) that in the event that one of the two countries suf-
fered an attack from a third power or powers the other country
undertook not to give aid or assistance to seid power or powers.
The treaty was to remain into effect for five years,
The Cabinet Information Bureau upon the announccment of
the treaty issued a statement declaring that the treaty made
a valuable contribution towards the progross of East Asia in
line with Japan's efforts to establish a "sphere of common
prosperity in Greater East Asia." It was intimated in the
statement that the treaty went beyond the scope of the con-
ventional non-aggression pact end that it was intended to be a
positive and not just a pro forma agreement between the two
countrios which would fit in with Japan's present international
policy.*

(2) Establishment of the Cabinet Information Board.
On December 6, the Cabinet Information Board came into
being. This Board was a consolidetion of the information
bureaus of the various Ministries under one organ. It was
to have addit ional duties, however, of dissemination of in-
formation end pronaganda, of control over the press, radio
broadcasting, and the motion pi:ture and thertrical enter-
tainments. Mr. Hobofumi Ito wes appointed head of the new
ureau.
(b) Reform of Civil Servicc System.
On December 31, the Privy Council finally gave its ap-
proval to the Government's plan for the reform of the Civil
Service system, and an ordinance enbodying the plan was sub-
mitted to the Emperor. For a number of years the Privy
Council had consistently declined to assent to the demands
made by previous governments for the revocation of the lew
guaranteeing the status of government officials. Under the
new ordinance th: Gov :rnment would be able to remove any
official from Government office, but it was understood that
the Privy Council had iven its consent to the change only
upon the assurance of the Cabinet that the Government would
refrain from promiscuously making removals or appointments.

(a) The New National Structure.

a structure for

in a mnner which would be in accord with the supreme aims
of the country. Industrial entormrises might be partitioned
into units or combined with other ontcrprises and control
would be exorcised over then to direct their assistance of
the Government. (3) In key industries, organizations or
so-called "control societies" composed of all the enter-
prises therein should be esteblished and their affeirs should
be conducted by directors or so-called "leaders" who were
to bo recommended by the enterprise and approved by tho Gov-
ernment. Th: Government itself would supervise these orgen-
izations.

VI.GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS*.
There was a decided increase in business and commercial
activity during December. The increase, however, was believed
to be almost entirely seasonal in view of the distribution of
year-end bonuses, year-end financial settlements, and heavy
retail sales in connection with the New Year's holiday:. A
sharp drop in the stock market occurred in the beginning of

General Economic Situation for the Year 1940.
Although the yeer l940, as far as information and figures
avuilable would show, represented a period of high industrial,
corwmeroial and agricultural activity, it witnessed a halt to the
trend of increased and expanding production of the previous
three years and a decline. in prosperity, The high level of
business

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.99)
-37-
business activity apparent last year which continued into Js≤C.
perhaps till the middle of the year, in any event gave way
during the last half of the year when definite signs of wea-
kening and contiaoion arpeared Both industrial and agrlcul-
tural output were pelow that for the previus ar.
0
The volume of industrial production was estimated to have
MME
declined by something in the neighborhood of 5 per cent slthough
its value in fact showed an increase as a result of the nigh-
er price level during the year. The causes for this decrease
C
were principally attributed to marked shortages of raw mate-
rials for both heavy and light industries, the shortag of
electric power during the early part and the closing months of
the year, and, with respect to light industries particularly,
the shrinkage of foreign markets for the manufactures. The
continued expansion of thc country's industrial production
capacity was, however, generally maintained, especially in the.
iron and steel, non-ferrous metal, machine tool and chemical
industries, but the rate of increase was considered to have
slowed down from that of the previous year.
The Government's statistics for the rice crop of Japan
Proper for the year was 60,874,000 koku (the koku is approxi-

than that for the previous year and was the largest ever harvest-

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Speech of the Forein Minister, Mr. Matsuoka
before the America-Japan Scciety. on Decenber
19, 1940,
On December 19, the Avericu-Janan Society held a luncheon
in honor of Admiral Kichisaburo Nomura, the newly appointed
Japanese Ambassador to the United States und  Lir. Horinouchi,
retiring Ambassador, and Mrs. Lorinouchi. The function, at
which Mr. Grew presided, was attended by a number of important
Japanese personages, and was perhaps the larrest gathering of
Japanese and American members ever to assemble at a meeting of
the Scciety.
Several of the hi hest Jepanese navel cfficers
wort pregent.
The Mhinister Por Foreir Lafaire, M. Yosuke
Matsuora. arlivered a spo ch d. she Luneheen on American-
Japarese affain: in w ich le propounded the position of Japan
vis-à-vis the Unitnt stuter under the present axioms of Japanese
forei n pljcy
In sumoary the sai tent points of the speech were as follows:
In begianiny the stoetantive part of the address the Foreigh
Minister ctated that is rust to adaittsd that the relations
botwoen Japan and the United States were severely strained.
The fundamental cause for this, he declared, was "American
misapprehens on of Jucen's aims and acpirations". He asserted
that sontrary, ovever, t Lpressicns current in America and
elsewhre Jupan vas engagel not un un imperialistic war of
sreed and agsrens on i China but in a noral crusade, endeavor-
ing to initlate an era  uduring peace in Greater East Asia.
The basic prirciples of Jepan stood unalterably for peace and
order. Japan "shut the door nuwhere and to none", and welcomed
any nation that desired to take a hand in the task at hand,
but

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
but upon the understand ing that under the "New Order" there
should be "no conquest, no oppression, no exploitation" by
those nations as well as on the part of Japan itself.
The Minister next spoke of Japan's aims to create
regional unity. He stated that the world was "too wide poli-
tically and too narrow economically"; that economic activity
should be world-wide and without linitation but that political
efforts should be restricted to only those spheres in which
a nation is vitally interested; and tlat if regional peace
were effectively secured the world, as a whole, would be able
to enjoy universel peace. He pointed to the recent Havana
Conference as a case in point.
Then taking up the question of the Tripartite Alliance he
asserted that Japan harbored no hostile intontion thereunder
toward America. "Japan", he said, "only desires to be left
elone so that it may carry on its constructive work unhindered
and on the other hand to see the trouble in China and the wor
in Europe brought speedily to an end without adding more parti-
cipants, particularly such a powerful one as Anericn." He
categorically stated that Japan would remain "loyal to her Allios
and that Jepan's foreign policy "will revolve, in the future,
around the Three Power Pact as its pivot."
Referring to the "China problom", he termod the fate of
China as "largely a question of sentiment to thc Americans" but
an issuo vital to the oxistonce of Japen. He said that Japan
was not without hope as to an early restoration of peace in
China" which would automatically liquidate tre tension between
Japan and America. He snid that the new Chinese Central Govern-
ment representod a "harbinger of better times" and he callod
upon

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
upon the United States to understand the broad aspects of the
situation and "join efforts with us in furthering our high aims".
After expressing a hope for the success of Admiral Nomura's
mission in bringing about an improverent in the relations be-
tween the two countries, Mr. Matsuoka condemned tho pessimists
and alarmists in both countries who believed a major Pacific
conflict inevitable and pleaded for common sense, cool hoaded-
ness and patience, whilo asking that Japan be givon time --
"a half century or even less" -- to prove itself to the world.
Following the Minister's speech the Ambassador rose and,
having extended the appreciation of the Socicty to Mr. Matsuoka,
expressed a few briof comments on the address. He stated: "In
onco moro expressing to His Excellency the Foreign Ministor the
appreciation of tho Society of his giving of his valuable time
to join us today, I wish to thank tho Minister on behalf of us
all for his onlightening address. My own comments on his ad-
dress will bc restricted to a minimum.
His Exccllency has brought out ccrtain points with which
wo must all agrcc, ospecially with Mr. Matsuoka's expressed
desire for peacc, his appcal for calm judgmont and his good
wishes for thc succcss of Admiral Nomura's mission. Other
points brought out wcrc of a controversial nature.
I cannot doal soverally with thosc points today. Yet
with all duc rospect I must rclievo tho Minister of his misap-
prehension that the intorest of thc Amorican pcoplo in China is
largoly sontimental. I an particularly glad to tako noto of
tho Minister's statomcnt that in tho Japanese program tho door
is to bo shut nowhoro and to no ono and that this program the
onvisagos no conquest, no opprossion, no oxploitation.
Tho Minister has lived long onough in the Unitod States
to know that tho Amorioan pcoplo aro fundamontally poaco-mindod
and

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
and furthermore that they stand for justice and equity. He
also knows that the American people are firmly determined on
certain matters among which on the one hard ars their obliga-
tions and on the other hand their rights. Their profoundest
wish is to see peace, prosperity, socurity, stebility and nappi-
ness assured to all nations.
In the present state of world affairs we must inevitably
realize that what counts in international relationships today,
and what we all have to face in formulating our several opinions,
is the concrete evidence of facts and actions, regardless of
the persuasive garb in which such facts and actions may be
dressed.
Let us say of nations as of men: "By their fruits ye
shall know them."
Adniral Nomura also delivered en address at the luncheon
which was of a non-political nature and which was given a very
favorable reception.
An identical version of Mr. Matsuoka's speech was pro-

nese version certain parts of the original text in English were
omitted, such as the expression of fervent hope for the success

of both peoples. No publicity whatever was given in the ver-

II. RELATIONS WITH OTIER COUNTRTES.
(a) Great Britain.
(1) German raiders in thc Pacific Ocean.
On December 5, the Norwegian ship "Ole Jacob" whish had
boen captured on her way from Singopore to Colombo by a Cerman
raider arrived at Kobe under a Germen prize crew. Toer. its
Norwegian crew and tho crew of another Norwegian ship that had
been captured and sunk in the Indian Occan, were tronsferred
aboard the German merchnt liner "Scharnhorst" end eventually
freed.
The "Ole Jacob" remaincd in port 36 hours and departed.
The arrival of this captured ship in Japan and tho move-
monts of a number of other G rmrn ships in Japanose waters gave
rise to suspicion that arrangoments were being made either to
fit out a German raider or that Japaneso ports werc bcing used
as beses from which to supply and provision Germon raiders in
the Pacific Occan.
Tho British Embassy made several approaches to the Japaneso
Foroign Offico in regerd to this matter. In reply to the British
Ambassador's inquirios the Vicc Minister for Foreign Affairs
stated that the "Ole Jacob" had cntered Kobe for supplies and
thnt upon invastigation no ovidence had boon f ound that she had
been usad as a rcidor or a supply ship to a reider. The British
Ambassador mde full reservations under international law as to
the use of ports of noutral countries by vessols of belligerent
countrios engaged in military activitios. Ho also pointed out
practical

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
practical means by which Japan might prevent such activities
as might result in an impairment of continued Allied shipping
to and from Japan and might possibly force Great Britain to
take precautionary measures with respect to Japanese vessels
in the Pacific Ocean, particularly in view of the fact that
German raiders had been believed to be using as disguises the
names and markings of Japanese ships. The Ambassador was in-
formed that the Japanese Government had no intention to permit
raiders to operate in Japanese waters or to be supplied from
Japanese territory.*
(2) British loan to the Chinese National
Government.
It will be recalled that the British Government in the
early part of December announced that a British loan of
£l0,Ooo,O0o was being made to the Chinese National Government.
On December 14, the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs brought
up the question of this loan during a conversation with the
British Ambassador. Mr. Ohashi strongly denounced the measure,
characterizing it as a further indication of the joint efforts
on the part of Great Britain and the United States to weaken
Japan, and he asserted that the declared intentions of the
British and American Governments initiating these actions in
the interests of China wcre mcre pretext and camouflage for the
implacable hostility of the United States and Great Britain
toward Jepan.**

noted

Great Britain, its nationals and their interests which had led

Editorial comment in Japan regarding the conclusion on
November 30 of the treaty between Japan and the "New Crinese
National Government" at Nanking was headed by a long t1eatise
on the subject issued by the Domei News Agency.
It desoribed
the treaty as laying the foundation for the "Muturl Prosperity
Sphere in Greater East Asia".
It then devoted its main atten-
tion to what it stated was the "international significance that
was to be attached to the event". It declared in this regard
that an end had been put to that phase of Chinese history
during which China had been denendent upon Great Britain and
the United States and that the shackles which those two countries
had imposed on China since the Opium Vars a hundred years ago
were at last broken. Henceforth, China, it held, would asso-
ciate itself with the signatories of the Tripartite Alliance
and could count upon the material and other support of the
Axis Powers. The conclusion of the treaty further sinified
more than anything else, it stated, the joint co-operation of
China and Japan against the nations that tried to stand in the
way of the construction of the "New Order in East Asia" and
elso joint endeavors for an early materialization of an "East
Asia Co-prosperity Sphere" as a preliminary step towards the
establishment of a new world order.
The vernacular press accorded an enthusiastic welcome to
the treaty. Typical of editorial comments were such statements
that "Sino-Japanese bonds had been tied into an inseparable
knot" and that a medium had been found whereby the disposal of
the China Incident would certainly be accelcrated. Much was
also made in the comments of the fact thet Japan "did not dosire
to take an inch of foreign territory nor to demand a single
cent in payment for tho last three and one half years of
hostilitios" as demonstrating the just and equitable attitude
of Japan towards China. Toguther with these comments the
editorials,

Much prominence was given in the Japanese press to the
announcement issued at Nanking on December 19 of the establish-
ment early in the coming year of a Central Bank of the New
National Government at Nanking to issue its own currency. The
Japanese Government explained that the establishment of a new
Central Bank was quite proper and natural and this attitude
was echoed in the Japanese press. It was stressed that one of
the motives for the issuance of a new currency was to "bring
out of hiding the idle native capital in the Yangtze region".
This capital which was estimated to be between four and six
billion yuan, it was stated, had not thus far participated in
the economic construction program formulated for Central China
and must be utilized for the economic development of the country.
The press also referred to the need of driving out of circula-
tion Chinese national currency as the latter was materially
assisting the continued resistance of the Chiang Kai-shek
regime. In order, however, not to cause a drastic disruption
of the present economic organization in China this latter aim,
it was warned, would have to be accomplished gradually.
(2) Military operations.*
Military engagements of only a minor scale between Japanese
forces and Chinese regular and guerilla troops were reparted
during the month as occurring in several areas in Central and
South China. One concerted action by a number of Japanese units
was announced to have taken place in the form of a "clean up"
campaign in Shansi Province against Chinese troops under General
Ma Chan Shan. The main scene of operations took place between
Lin Hsien and Hing Hsien. In the Nanchang area the Japanese
foroes made a small scale offensive resulting in the capture
at the end of the month of Tungyuon, 36 miles west of Nanohang.

was purely economic, "Japan has no territorial ambitions." In

Ishizo Kobayashi, on December 29, he vas quoted as stating that
"Japan's forein trade efforts hereafter must be concentrated
on the Netherlands East Indies and through the Indies, on
markets which India, Africa, Europe and America can offer."
(e) Theilnd.
On December 28 the treaty of amity between Japan and Thai-
land, signed on June 12, 1940, was promulgated and published in
the

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.98)
-26-
the Japenese Official Gazette. The treaty provided (1) for the
mutual respect of the territorial integrity of cach country and
the reaffirmation of peace and friendship cxisting between the
countries; (2) for the exchange of information end for con-
sultation on any question of cormon interest that might arise;
and (3) that in the event that one of the two countries suf-
fered an attack from a third power or powers the other country
undertook not to give aid or assistance to seid power or powers.
The treety was to remain into effect for five years,
The Cabinet Information Burenu upon the announccment of
the treaty issued a statement declaring that the treaty made
a valuable contribution towards the progress of East Asia in
line with Japan's efforts to establish a "sphere of common
prosperity in Greater East Asia." It was intimated in the
statement that the treaty went beyond the scope of the con-
vontional non-aggression pact end that it was intended to be a
positive and not just a pro forma agreement between the two
countrios which would fit in with Japan's present international
policy.*
Reference is made to the foregoing section - Relations
With French Indochina - with respect to Jepnnese interest in
tho dispute between Thailand and French Indochina over the

the end of December the new treaty had not been negotiated
between the two countries and the protocol itself lapsed with
no agreement apparently having been reached for its further
extension. The press generally took an optimistic view of the
matter anticipating that at least the protocol would be extended
and that there was still the possibility of concluding a
favorable general political arrangement with the Soviet Union.
Some sections of the press were, however, pessimistic and
presented the view that the policy of the Soviet Union was
motivated partly by opportunistic considerations. It was
felt that the Kremlin was at present quite confident of its
international power and that therefore it did not show such
cordiality and friendship towards Japan as it had during the
previous winter and summer when with the emergency in Europe
it was endeavoring to recover lost territory along the western
frontier and when it was most anxious to protect its security
on its eastern border. It was noteworthy that the entire press
took a very restrained and cautious attitude in its comments
upon Sovict-Japanese relations.
The Embassy was informed that the negotiations regarding
the conclusion of some sort of far-reaching political arrange-
ment, such as, possibly, a non-aggression pact between Russia and
Japan, had not made any headway.*. It was understood that Russia
was domanding a very high price, possibly one that would be too
high for Japan to pay, for a new fisheries agreement or a poli-
tical arrangement between the two countries.**

Hiroshi Oshima had been appointed as Japanese Ambassador to
Germany. It will be recalled that General Oshima had formerly
been Japanese Ambassador to Berlin but in consequence of the
signing of the Soviet-German non-aggression pact and trade
agreements in August 1939, he was recalled during the autumn of
that year. General Oshima is known to entertain strong Axis
leanings and to be a close associate of Mr. Toshio Shiratori,
former Japanese Ambassador to Italy and a present advisor to
the Foreign Minister. His reappointment, therefore, as
Ambassador to Berlin accords with Japan's present foreign
policy under the Tripartite Alliance.
On December 21 it was also disclosed in the press that
Lieutenant General Tomoyuki Yamashita, Inspector General of
Army Aviation and concurrently Director General of the Military
Aviation Headquarters, would head a military mission to Germany
and Italy "to study conditions of the present European war on
the spot". It was further reported that special attention would
be given to the study of air forces, mechanized equipment and
new implements of warfare and the strategy of the employment of
such equipment. The mission departed on December 22, 1940.*
There was manifested, during the month under review, a
further intensification of Japan's rapprochement with Germany
not only in official utterances but in other respects. In a
press interview, the spokesman of the Cabinet Information
Bureau referred to a statement issued by the German Foreign
Office on December 2l concerning Germany's view regarding the
inoreased American assistance to Britain. Mr. Ishii stated
that the Japanese people could understand and appreciate fully
"the forbearance hitherto shown by the German people, and that
the patience of (ormany seems to have been taxed almost to the
timate point".

(2) Establishment of the Cabinet Informetion Board.
On December 6, the Cabinet Information Board came into
being. This Board was a consolidation of the information
bureaus of the various Ministries under one organ. It was
to have additional duties, however, oi dissemination of in-
formation end propaganda, of control over the press, radio
broadcasting, and the motion picture and thertrical enter-
tainments. Mr. liobofuni Ito wes appointed head of the new
bureau.
(b) Reform of Civil Service System.
On December 3l, the Privy Council finally gave its ap-
proval to the Government's plan for the reform of the Civil
Service system, and an ordinance enbodying the plan was sub-
mitted to the Emperor. For a number of years the Privy
Council had consistently declined to assent to the demands
mede by previous governments for the revocation of the lew
guaranteeing the status of government officials. Under the
new orinance th: Gov :rnment would be able to remove any
official from Government office, but it was understood that
the Privy Council had fiven its consent to the change only
upon the assurance of the Cabinet that the Government would
refrain from promiscuously making removals or appointments.

a structure for

of the country. Industrial entermrises might be partitioned

would be exorcised over then to direct their assistanco of
the Governmont. (3) In key industries, organizations or
so-called "control societies" composed of all the enter-
prises therein should bo esteblished and their affeirs should
be conducted by directors or so-called "leaders" who were
to bo recommended by the enterprise and approved by thc Gov-
ernment. Th: Government itself would supervise these orgen-
izations.

V.
GOVERIIENT FINANCE.
National bond issuss in the amount of Yen 1,300,000,000
were offered durinf the month under review. The absorption
of the bonds was reported to have been exoeptionally low, a-
mounting to less than 30 per cent. This noor rate may in
part be attributed to the very large amount of bonds issued,
and also in part explained by the fact that money was in
strong demand by private business in connection with the year-
end activity. Total Government bonds floated during the year
amounted

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.98)
-35
amounted to Yon 6,684,000:000 as against ar nount of Yen
5,298,000,000 issued during 1939. At the ond of 194c the
national bonded indebtedness had roached vhe figuro of 'on
28,253,209,000 including Yen 1,245,055,000 in furcicn bonds.
The note issues of the Bank of Japan increased by the
last day of the month to the unprccodented amount of Yon
4,930,000,000. This figure was grcntor by over one billion
Yen than that for the corresponding day of the provious year.

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.99)
The Government's statistics for the rice crop of Japan
Proper for the year was 60,874,000 koku (the koku is approxi-
mately 5 bushels) representing a ll.7 per cent decrease as
compared to the crop of last year and a 6.6 per cent decrease
compared with the average during the last five years. The
barley crop also decreased but the wheat crop was much greater
than that for the previous year and was the largest ever harvest-
ed. The l940 cccoon output was officially reported to have
amounted to 8',540.c00 kwan (one kwan equaling 8.3 pounds) which
although above the average for the past five years represented
a decline of 3.6 per cent from that of 1939. The value of this
crop, according to the Government figures, was about the same as
that of 1939, despite the drop in raw silk prices from the poak
of

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.99)
-38-
of Yen 2,425 per bale in January to an average price of around
Yen 1,350 per bale during the last six months of the year. The
value of leading crops which account for approximately seventy-
five per cent of Jepan's agricultural income was estimated to
have registered a decrease of 9 per cent for the yeur in compa-
rison to that of the previous year. The Government's plans to
increase agricultural production during 1940 were not succes-
ful due to the lack of sufficient fertilizer, farm implements and
materials, and shortage of labor, while the extension of state
price and other control over agricultural products contributed
much toward the curtailment of the farm income.
The upward trend of the general price level, so pronounced
during tho latter part of 1939, was checked in 1940 and showed
even a downward tendency arter Septomber. According to official
statistics the cost of living rose by 10.5 per cent from De-
cember 1939 to Decomber 1940.

Imports Yen 3,709,000,000
Tho favoreble balance was thus Yen 263,000,000. The figures
represonted an increase of 1 per cent in exports and 19 per
cent in imports in veluo above those of the previous year.
The
figures included trade with the "yen bloc" and no suparate

ple by 1960 for Japan proper. It was hoped
ould be attained.

ry marking a large inorease over the

ral accounts was presented to the Diet.

normal and seasonal but was also attributed
to uncertainties of Diet deliberations, exten-

together with a ourtailment of production of

oipated.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) General Relations - Press Comment and
Statements by Government Officials.
Summary
The month of Jenuary was marked by an atmosphere of
extreme pessimism with regard to the future course of
Anerican-Japanese relations and by a growing feeling that
serious difficulty between the two countries was inevitable.
There appeared to be no room for compromise between Japan's
policy of creating a 'New Order" and of cooperation with
the Axis Powers and American foreign policy as the two were
irreconcilable. Intentional hostility to Japan was read
into the United States' program of aid to Great Britain
and China and the American Government was considered to
be leading the United States into war in Europe which would
spread to the Pacific and result in a world-wide confiagration.
The Japanese press was invective and recriminatory in its
attitude towards the United States while at the same time
it continued to appeal for reconsideration of American
policy and for an endeavor on the part of the United States
to understand "Japan's true aims and actions". Peace with
the United States was generally desired by the country but
it was questioned whether Japan had not already gono so
far that it would be worsc to turn back from its aims than
to press on to their fulfillment. The Foreign Minister
admitted the seriousness of the situation, stating that
without American understanding of Japanese claims there
was no hope for improvoment of American-Japanese relations
and declared that Japan might have to make a "supreme
decision" in consecuenco of British and Americen activities
in the Orient. Tho foeling was current of a confidence in
the

=== Page 539 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
the ability of Japan and its Axis Allics successfully to
combat any opposition. At the same time a campaign vas
instituted by sections of the press and officicl ciicles
to build up a front against the Unitod Statos and to assure
the nation that Japan's expansionist asnirations might be
fulfilled without serious risk that thc United States would
tako measures in opposition thoreto other than those of an
economic nature.*
Comment Upon Prssident Roosev lt's "Firesidc"
Broadcast on Ducumber 29 and Messago to Congress

Press conment upon the President's "fireside" broed-

6 was markodly bitter and pointed. In the nunurous cditor-
ials devoted to the sub ject tho viw was unenimousl y takon
that the messeces represented on the part of the Arurison
Govornment an openly hostile and challenging intent toverds
Japan. It was asserted that the Adninistration had mbarked
upon a policy of abandoning American neutrality end thot
the assistance which the Covernment was bent upon giving
Great Britain and the Chinese National Government amounted
in effect to virtual, although indircct, wer on the Axis
Powers. The opinion was freely voiced that it would be
merely a matter of time before the Unitcd States entered
the European war, in which case hostilitics would most
likely sproad to the Pacific Occan. It was argued that the
United States could justly be condemned as tho aggressor
if it proceoded to implement fully a policy of opposing
the crontion of a "Now Ordor" in the world, particularly
with respect to the Far East where Japen held by right the

Lease-Lend Bill.
The announcement of the int roduction into the Congress
of the "Bill to Aid Defense of Derocracies" or the "Lease-
Lend Bill" was carried in all the Japanese newspapers under
such headlines as "Dictatorship" and "U.S. Entry into War".
Comments of the press regarding the bill were in the main
to the effect that its passage meant certain participation
of the United States in the European war, probably in the
spring of this year, and that this would result in turn in
a crisis on the Pacific of "far greater proportions than
that on the Atlantic".**
Press comment reached a high point of invective, re-
proach and recrimination in its treatment of the testimony
given by Secretary Hull before the House Foreign Relations
Committee on January 15 on the "Lease-Lend Bill". The
editorials referred to the Secretary's remarks in such
terms as their being "a mass of malicious and spiteful
abuse and attacks". The Secretary was condemned for  s-
torting facts, ignoring realities in East Asia and creating
illusions of Japan's threats.
In contesting the Secretary's statement the counter
charge was made that it was the United States which had
been unfriendly, as evidenced by its abrogation of the
Treaty of Commerce and Navigation in order to restrain
Britain

Propaganda Campaign Regarding American-Japanese
Relations.
In addition to the general editorial comment upon
Americcn-Japanese relations there appoared in the Japanese
press a series of feature articles and reports which to a
large oxtent were of an inspired cast und of a propagande
nature.
Writings by retiring navel officers and conientators
on navel affairs set forth the viow that the United States
Navy could not fight in Oriental waters with any prospect
of success, that the United States would not dare to send
its fleet to the Fer East and that in any event the Japanese
Navy was as good, if not supcrior, to that of the United
States. Opinions were voiced by other writers that the
United States would not institute hostilities against Japan
7119d
but would merely continue to resort to bringing economic
pressure against Japn. It was further predicted that the
United Statcs was not likely to enter the European war for
the time being and that the possibility of Japan's bein
drawn into e confl ict with the United States through its
obligations under the Tripartite Allirnce could be minimized.
These observations were supplemented by the contention that
American-Japanese relations had renched an impasse through
complete

(a) Dict.
On Januury 21 tlh. Diet was convcned in regular annual
sossicn. The popular name given to this Dict Session was
"The Imperial Rule Assistance Sossion". The first day, as
was the usual practice, was devoted to the doliverance of
speeches by the Government's ministers. The Prime Minister
expounded the Ceneral administrative policies of the Govern-
ment while the speeches of the War and Navy Minister con-
sisted of reports on the war situation. The speech of the
Foreign Minister dealt solely with Japan's forcin rela-
tions.** After reviewing the economic conditions of Japan
during the year 1940, the Finance Minister in his speech
pointed out that the present year would be frought with
many difficulties which would require all the concerted
efforts of Japan to overcome. He mentioned that recent
developments in the international situation had had a con-
siderable effect upon the domestic economy of Japan, espe-
cially with regard to the supply of materials. He declared
that a number of read justments would have to be made in
the nation's economic structure and that there might be a
temporary decrease in the supply of goods for other than
official

The House of Representatives in the second day of the
session adopted a resolution to prohibit all interpellations
of the speeches of the cabinet ministers.
Thereafter in-

ment would refrain from introducin3 a nuaber of controver-
sial bills in this session of the Diet.
The most important
of the bills that were cancelled were the Industrial Orga-

economic struc ture of Japan, the Election Law Reform Bill,

Control Bill, and the Prefectural Governnonts Teform Bill.
In all only one half of the llo bills that were originally
intended to bo subnitted to the Diet were introduced.
Arong the important bills submitted to the Dict were:
a bill for revision of the National Guneral Mobilization
Law, the National Defense Security Bill, a bill for the
reform of the Peace Maintainance Law, and the Exchange Con-
trol Bill.
